| **[Who Says What: Symbolic Trimodal Binding Mechanisms in Audio-Visual LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31193v2)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current Audio-Visual LLMs (AVLLMs) struggle with reasoning over videos featuring multi-speaker dialogues. In such videos, resolving "who says what" is crucial, which necessitates trimodal (text-audio-visual) binding. Motivated by these challenges, we systematically investigate how this trimodal binding is achieved in AVLLMs. Specifically, we identify emergent symbolic trimodal binding mechanisms in AVLLMs that utilize modality-specific symbolic variables. By encoding auditory and visual components into symbolic variables-capturing temporal utterance sequences and spatial entity coordinates, respectively-the model establishes cross-modal linking within this abstract space. Crucially, we reveal that when trimodal binding fails, the breakdown predominantly stems from misaligned audio-visual connections. To overcome this bottleneck, we introduce an audio-visual prompting method utilizing an off-the-shelf Active Speaker Detection (ASD) model. By simply overlaying visual bounding boxes on active speakers, this training-free approach yields immediate performance gains across four conversation-centric benchmarks. Moreover, lightweight fine-tuning of fewer than 300 steps on these ASD-prompted-videos extends these gains to three general AV benchmarks, suggesting the generalizability of our method. Our code is available at https://github.com/kaistmm/Who-Says-What.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Reaching Is Not Finding: Map-Anchored Visual Prompting for the Last Mile of Aerial Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11510v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aerial vision-language navigation (VLN) asks a drone to find an object described in natural language. The description usually locates the object relative to named landmarks, such as streets and buildings. We observe that current navigators often bring the drone close to the target and then stop at the wrong place: they reach the target without finding it. This gap is shared by navigators of very different designs, from imitation learners to a fine-tuned 7B vision-language model. A simple test reveals the cause. When we replace the target object in the instruction with another one next to the same landmarks, no navigator goes to it, and most stop where they did before. Navigators learn to reach the landmarks, not to identify the described object. We therefore propose MAP-VP, a plug-and-play module for the last step. After any navigator stops, MAP-VP draws the landmarks named in the instruction onto the drone's current view, using the landmark outlines that the benchmark provides on a map. A fine-tuned 4B vision-language model then locates the target relative to these landmarks. The training data are generated automatically from the benchmark annotations. Neither drawing the landmarks nor fine-tuning helps much alone, but together they are highly effective. Without retraining any navigator, MAP-VP significantly improves all six navigators we test on CityNav. It roughly doubles the success rate of the two strongest trained navigators, raising HETT from 25.9% to 54.6% and FlightGPT from 24.2% to 48.9%, whereas CityNav's official detector-based last step adds less than two points to HETT.</p></details> |  |
| **[Attribute-Evidence Learning with Multimodal Prompt for Generalized Category Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12975v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generalized category discovery (GCD) aims to discover novel categories from unlabeled data by leveraging limited labeled samples from old classes. Existing methods that rely solely on visual cues often neglect the multimodal nature of human perception, prompting recent approaches to incorporate textual information such as category tags and attributes. However, these approaches typically entangle global tags and local attributes within a shared global image representation, rendering attributes unreliable and potentially providing misleading cues that can drive the model toward incorrect predictions. To this end, we propose AptM-GCD, a novel multimodal framework that exploits apt textual guidance through attribute evidence learning and multimodal prompt reasoning. Specifically, we design an Attribute-Evidence Learning (AEL) mechanism that treats attributes as localized visual evidence rather than global category descriptors. By interacting attribute embeddings with patch-level visual tokens, AEL identifies reliable attribute-related regions and extracts discriminative local evidence, thereby reducing the impact of noisy or irrelevant attribute cues. Building upon the extracted evidence, we further develop a Multimodal Prompt Learning (MPL) mechanism to bridge local attribute semantics and global visual understanding. MPL converts fine-grained textual evidence into visual prompts and injects them into a multimodal prompt transformer, enabling attribute information to enhance category discrimination through contextual reasoning without enforcing direct global alignment between modalities. Extensive experiments on six datasets demonstrate that AptM-GCD consistently achieves SoTA performance across both fine-grained and generic benchmarks. Moreover, the proposed framework exhibits strong generalization ability, delivering consistent gains under unimodal and class-imbalanced GCD settings.</p></details> | <details><summary>IEEE ...</summary><p>IEEE Transactions on Pattern Analysis and Machine Intelligence (TPAMI)</p></details> |
| **[Graph Visualizations in Prompts for Knowledge Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12938v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study when and how multimodal input improves knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) in large vision--language models. Using a controlled evaluation over 1,500 WebQSP and CWQ questions and six VLMs within a fixed KGQA pipeline, we compare text-only prompting against text-plus-graph visualization at the per-question level and analyze performance changes as a function of reasoning complexity, model size, and image layout. Adding a graph image yields an average gain of 1.85 F1 points, driven by strong precision improvements on low-complexity questions (+4.65 F1). Mixed-effects and regression analyses show that these multimodal gains do not depend strongly on model scale, but are highly sensitive to graph layout: image-only settings sharply degrade performance, hierarchical Graphviz layouts outperform less organized renderings, and decomposing large subgraphs into separated paths yields the largest improvements. Taken together, the results indicate that visual prompting primarily aids KGQA by enhancing prediction precision in low-complexity contexts, provided that visual design respects cognitive and perceptual constraints. To optimize both accuracy and inference efficiency, we argue for adaptive visual prompting policies that selectively append diagrams to small, visually sparse subgraphs with well-structured layouts, avoiding unnecessary visual clutter and token overhead on complex queries. We conclude by relating these findings to a visual notation design theory, outlining how principled diagram encoding can guide future multimodal VLM prompting.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at AACL-IJCNLP conference</p></details> |
| **[StreamGaze: Gaze-Guided Temporal Reasoning and Proactive Understanding in Streaming Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.01707v4)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Streaming video understanding requires models not only to process temporally incoming frames, but also to anticipate user intent for realistic applications such as Augmented Reality (AR) glasses. While prior streaming benchmarks evaluate temporal reasoning, none measure whether Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can interpret or leverage human gaze signals within a streaming setting. To fill this gap, we introduce StreamGaze, the first benchmark designed to evaluate how effectively MLLMs utilize gaze for temporal and proactive reasoning in streaming videos. StreamGaze introduces gaze-guided past, present, and proactive tasks that comprehensively assess streaming video understanding. Past and present tasks require immediate responses based on observations available at query time, whereas proactive tasks require models to continuously process incoming frames and respond at the appropriate future moment. To build StreamGaze, we develop a gaze-video Question Answering (QA) generation pipeline that aligns egocentric videos with raw gaze trajectories through fixation extraction, region-specific visual prompting, and scanpath construction. This pipeline produces spatio-temporally grounded QA pairs that reflect human perceptual dynamics. Across all StreamGaze tasks, we observe substantial performance gaps between state-of-the-art MLLMs and human performance, highlighting key limitations in gaze-based temporal reasoning, intent modeling, and proactive prediction. We further provide detailed analyses of gaze prompting strategies, reasoning behaviors, and task-specific failure modes, offering insights into current limitations and directions for future research. All data and code are publicly available to support research in gaze-guided streaming video understanding.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the 40th Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026)</p></details> |
| **[STARE: Scene Text-Aware multimodal Retrieval for Everyday objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06089v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this study, we focus on service robots that retrieve and transport everyday objects based on free-form instructions that may refer to visual features and scene text. This is challenging because it requires a multimodal understanding of the visual features and spatial relationships of objects, as well as scene text embedded in the environment. To address this, we propose Scene Text-Aware multimodal Retrieval for Everyday objects (STARE), which leverages visual and textual cues in response to free-form instructions. STARE introduces Crosslingual Visual Prompt to enhance scene text interpretation, and models the complex relationships between named entities in the instructions and their corresponding objects. We evaluate STARE using two novel benchmarks, GoGetIt and TextCaps-test, both of which consist of real-world indoor/outdoor images and instructions involving object manipulation and navigation. The results show that STARE outperforms all baseline methods on standard multimodal retrieval metrics. Furthermore, in physical experiments conducted under a zero-shot transfer setting, STARE achieves an overall success rate of 80%. Our project page and code are provided in the Supplementary Materials.</p></details> | <details><summary>ACCV ...</summary><p>ACCV 2026 MM4SciReal Workshop</p></details> |
| **[Test-time Correction: An Online 3D Detection System via Visual Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.07768v4)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper introduces Test-time Correction (TTC), an online 3D detection system designed to rectify test-time errors using various auxiliary feedback, aiming to enhance the safety of deployed autonomous driving systems. Unlike conventional offline 3D detectors that remain fixed during inference, TTC enables immediate online error correction without retraining, allowing autonomous vehicles to adapt to new scenarios and reduce deployment risks. To achieve this, we equip existing 3D detectors with an Online Adapter (OA) module -- a prompt-driven query generator for real-time correction. At the core of OA module are visual prompts: image-based descriptions of objects of interest derived from auxiliary feedback such as mismatches with 2D detections, road descriptions, or user clicks. These visual prompts, collected from risky objects during inference, are maintained in a visual prompt buffer to enable continuous correction in future frames. By leveraging this mechanism, TTC consistently detects risky objects, achieving reliable, adaptive, and versatile driving autonomy. Extensive experiments show that TTC significantly improves instant error rectification over frozen 3D detectors, even under limited labels, zero-shot settings, and adverse conditions. We hope this work inspires future research on post-deployment online rectification systems for autonomous driving.</p></details> |  |
| **[SeeNav-Agent: Enhancing Vision-Language Navigation with Visual Prompt and Step-Level Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.02631v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) agents based on Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) often suffer from errors of perception, reasoning, and planning, which significantly hinder their navigation performance. To address these limitations, a novel VLN agent framework, named SeeNav-Agent, is proposed. First, to reduce hallucinations of the visual module of the VLN agent, a dual-view Visual Prompt (VP) technique is introduced, which can also improve the agent's understanding of current spatial states. Subsequently, a novel step-level Reinforcement Fine-Tuning (RFT) method, Step Reward Group Policy Optimization (SRGPO), is designed for post-training. In SRGPO, we first define verifiable process rewards for navigation, and then perform efficient step-level advantage estimation by randomly grouping navigation steps. SRGPO provides dense reward signals for the RFT of the VLN agent and enhances its planning capability. Experiments on both the EmbodiedBench Navigation (Embench-Nav) and VLN-CE validate the effectiveness of the proposed method. By introducing the zero-shot VP module, the GPT-4.1 achieves a navigation success rate of 86.7%, surpassing the current best LVLM by approximately 20 percentage points (pp) on Embench-Nav and achieves an 11.1 pp improvement on VLN-CE compared to the base model. With VP+SRGPO, the Qwen2.5-VL-3B model reaches a navigation success rate of 72.3%, outperforming the best existing LVLM model by 5.6 pp on Embench-Nav and achieves 8.9 pp improvement on VLN-CE compared to the baseline method. Moreover, compared to GRPO and GiGPO, the proposed SRGPO demonstrates significant improvements in training stability, convergence efficiency, and generalization capability.</p></details> | 15 pages,4 figures |
| **[ZeroDex: Zero-Shot Long-Horizon Dexterous Manipulation via Multi-View 3D-Grounded VLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19340v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present ZeroDex, a zero-shot framework for long-horizon dexterous manipulation that grounds language instructions into executable 3D task plans from calibrated multi-view RGB images. Our framework builds on the grounding ability that vision-language models (VLMs) already exhibit in 2D and lifts their predictions into 3D through multi-view fusion, which extends VLM grounding beyond image-space keypoints to the metric 3D quantities that manipulation actually requires: contact points, placement targets, and tool-motion anchors. Because execution is driven by these 3D groundings rather than a learned policy, the system preserves the VLM's open-world generalization, without collecting demonstrations or post-training for a specific task or embodiment. The VLM decomposes a complex instruction into a sequence of atomic tasks, each executed by a primitive from a small reusable library that covers manipulation tasks including pick-and-place and tool-use: for tool-use, we retrieve an object-centric atomic action corresponding to the inferred skill category and align its stored 6D tool trajectory to the grounded keypoints; for dexterous execution, we expand the lifted grasp keypoint into a task-conditioned grasp affordance region and generate feasible grasp-motion pairs with an arm-hand motion generator. Real-world experiments show improved 3D grounding accuracy and higher task success than zero-shot visual-prompting and constraint-based baselines, both of which ground from a single RGB-D view, and our demonstration-free system outperforms VLA policies fine-tuned on 100 task demonstrations. We further demonstrate long-horizon manipulation, where execution verification and replanning recover from intermediate failures, and show that the system generalizes to unseen objects and tool-use tasks in novel scenes.</p></details> |  |
| **[SciFigBench: A Benchmark for Scientific Method Figure Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27087v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific method figures often provide researchers with a clear visual entry point into a paper's methodology and core contributions, yet their dense layouts and complex information flows are difficult for Vision Language Models (VLMs) to interpret. Existing benchmarks predominantly focus on coarse-grained tasks, such as context-based retrieval or holistic comprehension, overlooking the fine-grained content and inter-module relations within complex methodological diagrams. To address these overlooked aspects, we introduce Scientific Figure Benchmark (SciFigBench), a benchmark for evaluating core capabilities required for scientific method figure understanding. Specifically, it targets three capabilities: dense content perception, visual relation comprehension, and information-flow tracing. For this purpose, we curate 4,352 scientific method figures from real-world research papers and construct 40,670 QA pairs to operationalize these capabilities as measurable question-answering tasks. All annotations are generated by dedicated pipelines and then manually verified. We further propose SciFigCue, a training-free visual prompting module that externalizes arrow-induced dependencies as visual relational evidence for method-figure reasoning. Experiments across open-source and proprietary VLMs show that even strong models remain substantially limited in visual relation comprehension, while SciFigCue improves performance across several tasks without parameter updates. The code and related resources are available at https://github.com/LeNcyer/SciFigBench.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by EMNLP 2026 Main Conference. Code and benchmark resources: https://github.com/LeNcyer/SciFigBench</p></details> |
| **[Imagine Before You Draw: Visual Prompt Engineering for Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04457v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Incorporating visual semantic representations as an intermediate step before image generation can reduce the modeling difficulty between text and images, thereby improving generation quality. Recent works such as X-Omni and BLIP3o-Next have explored this direction, but they typically use a two-stage external pipeline: a separate autoregressive model first generates semantic tokens, which are then fed as conditioning to an independent diffusion decoder. When the decoder is conditioned only on the compressed semantic plan, it cannot directly access the original input, which introduces an information bottleneck that limits detail preservation in downstream tasks such as editing. Internal architectures such as Transfusion, BAGEL, and Show-o2 avoid this bottleneck by enabling cross-modal interaction within a single model, but they still face the difficult text-to-pixel modeling gap without intermediate semantic guidance. We propose Visual Prompt Engineering (VPE), which can be seamlessly integrated into such internal frameworks. Specifically, the model first autoregressively generates visual semantic tokens ($e.g.$, SigLIP 2) as "visual prompts" that capture the semantic layout, then generates the full image tokens conditioned on this plan. We validate VPE across class-conditional generation, text-to-image generation, and image editing, covering various token types and model architectures. Results show that VPE can accelerate convergence, raise quality ceilings, and through internal integration, achieve substantially better editing preservation (PSNR: $26.76$ vs. $19.92$) than a matched external pipeline with semantic-only decoder conditioning, while maintaining competitive editing responsiveness.</p></details> |  |
| **[Benchmarking Multimodal Large Language Models for Zero-Shot Human-Object Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18161v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong zero-shot reasoning across diverse domains, but their potential for Human-Object Interaction (HOI) remains unexplored. Unlike traditional HOI approaches that rely on extensive labeled datasets and fixed interaction taxonomies, MLLMs offer flexible reasoning, making them well-suited for dynamic, real-world scenarios. In this work, we present the first systematic study of MLLMs for HOI recognition and detection within a pipeline that combines an external object detector for entity localization with zero-shot multimodal reasoning for interaction understanding. We introduce a benchmark framework for principled zero-shot evaluation, consisting of (i) a unified taxonomy of zero-shot prompting strategies, and (ii) a methodology that transforms token-level logits into interaction scores, enabling fair comparison with specialized HOI models under standard metrics. To support prompt-based evaluation, we additionally construct new question-answer annotations for standard HOI datasets, transforming them into a format suitable for MLLMs. Building on this, we propose two visual prompting strategies, namely Objects as Context and Black Other Objects, that guide MLLMs to target objects while preserving scene context. Experiments on HICO, HICO-DET, and V-COCO demonstrate state-of-the-art zero-shot performance, achieving 53.50 mAP on HICO and 23.69 mAP on HICO-DET. Overall, our work provides a principled benchmarking framework for MLLMs in HOI, opening the door to new models and datasets and enabling flexible open-world interaction understanding.</p></details> |  |
| **[Mind the Temporal Gap: Evaluating Video Models for Temporal Understanding in Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12575v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern video-language models are increasingly treated as general-purpose video reasoners, yet it remains unclear whether they robustly capture temporal dynamics in safety-critical settings such as driving. We study this question through controlled ego-motion tasks that require identifying actions from driving videos. Across video-language models, we find that standard prompting, visual prompting, few-shot prompting, and chain-of-thought prompting remain unreliable and highly task- and model-dependent, with persistent class biases, although newer models show substantial task-specific few-shot gains. In contrast, lightweight LoRA finetuning and simple probes trained on intermediate visual features recover strong motion-discriminative performance, indicating that motion-relevant cues are often present inside the models but are not reliably used by the final video-to-language pathway. These results suggest that, in driving, current video-language models remain unreliable temporal reasoners and often behave more like frame-level, bias-driven recognizers than dependable temporal world models.</p></details> |  |
| **[Seeing the Invisible: Physics-Guided Visual Prompting for Temperature- and Radiation-Aware VLA Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07558v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have become a major paradigm for Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN). However, in safety-critical facilities, invisible risks such as radiation or temperature spikes cannot be detected by an RGB camera, and handling each risk is expensive, requiring a new encoder, new data, and model retraining. We propose Physics-Guided Visual Prompting (PG-VP), a plug-and-play multimodal perception module that instead reuses what a frozen VLA model already does well: avoiding visible obstacles. Given a proximal radiation or thermal source, PG-VP performs a physics-guided risk assessment to determine the avoidance direction and overlays a corresponding virtual obstacle that moves across consecutive frames (Dynamic Visual Prompting). The navigation policy then naturally detours around this invisible hazard. The identical virtual obstacle is used regardless of hazard type, so the visual prompting pattern remains fixed as sensors are added. When no hazard is detected, nothing is rendered, and the policy behaves exactly as it would without PG-VP. We evaluate PG-VP on OmniNav using the val-unseen splits of R2R-CE and RxR-CE, where it guides the policy toward intended low-risk actions in 84.9% and 83.2% of cases, at a cost of 6.8 and 7.9 percentage points in navigation success rate. We further test it with distinct scenarios on a real robot in the presence of actual thermal and radiation sources, all without any retraining. The real test shows that PG-VP effectively avoids these invisible hazards, improving worst-10% average trajectory safety by 63.45% and 32.59% against thermal and radiation sources, respectively.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 7 figures, 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[SetOPD: From Few Visual Exemplars to Multimodal Candidate Sets for Remote-Sensing Open-Prompt Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32529v1)** | 2026-09-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-prompt detectors allow users to specify targets with text, visual exemplars, or both. We argue that existing designs underuse the visual modality in two ways. First, multiple exemplars are commonly compressed into a single class-level embedding. This textualizes visual prompting: the resulting vector plays the role of another class name, is often aligned to or injected into the text pathway, and may be suboptimal when only a few heterogeneous exemplars are available. Second, existing methods interact primarily in prompt or representation space, before modality-specific detection states are formed. We address both issues from a set perspective: \setopd preserves modality-specific decoding states from a shared prompt-conditioned initialization and performs explicit multimodal collaboration at the candidate-state level. For the first issue, we introduce \br prompting, which reads every boxed exemplar in its full scene context and decomposes the pooled support evidence into a Base anchor and a learned Residual correction; the resulting prompt has fixed capacity regardless of the number of examples and drives its own visual detection pathway. For the second, we recast text--visual collaboration from representation-level fusion into a candidate-set modeling problem. Paired-Query Arbitration (\pqa) then performs explicit cross-modal state arbitration only after modality-specific candidate states have been formed. The two readers share query initialization so their candidates are paired by index; a learned gate arbitrates within each pair, followed by a permutation-equivariant module that reasons over the fused set.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Plot Twist: Jailbreaking Unified Multimodal Models with a Three-Act NarrativeAttack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26473v2)** | 2026-09-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Unified Multimodal Understanding and Generation Models (UMMs) increasingly combine visual understanding and image generation within a single interactive workflow, making generated visual content available as later reasoning context. However, existing jailbreak evaluations mostly study text rewriting or isolated visual prompts, leaving the safety risk of narrative cross-turn visual grounding underexplored. We propose NarrativeAttack, a semantic-preserving visual narrative jailbreak framework. NarrativeAttack employs a three-act narrative structure in which the UMM's own generator produces images for the setup (pre-event) and resolution (post-event) stages, making the full attack workflow self-contained while concealing the malicious event as a hidden climax. The attack concludes with an image-based "guessing game" that embeds the original malicious query among benign candidates, compelling the model to select and answer the most relevant one based on the established narrative context. A dynamic difficulty mechanism further enhances attack stability. Experiments show NarrativeAttack consistently surpasses prior approaches, achieving up to 88.25% ASR on Gemini-2.5-Flash. These results uncover an underdeveloped vulnerability and highlight the urgent need for safety alignment in UMMs.</p></details> | EMNLP 2026 Findings |
| **[Topology-Informed Visual Prompting For Vision Language Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23944v1)** | 2026-09-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language-action (VLA) policies can struggle with manipulation tasks with complex obstacle geometries due to partial observability. These complex geometries can lead to similar visual observations or robot configurations requiring qualitatively different actions, a distinction that can be quantified using topological signatures. While motion planners with full knowledge of environment geometries and object states can reason about these signatures in planning, this information is often not known at deployment. To address this issue, we present a topology-guided visual-prompting framework that uses simulation-based planning to augment a nominal demonstration dataset and provides vision-based guidance at deployment. Our method uses a Gauss-Linking-Integral topological signature representation to capture important topological properties of the environment. Using privileged geometry information from a simulation approximation of our environment, we augment a VLA fine-tuning dataset with trajectories that move the system to a demonstrated signature and, from the new configuration, resume task execution. A vision-language model (VLM) is fine-tuned on the same dataset to both predict signatures from live camera observations and predict end-effector waypoints, which are rendered as visual prompts on the observations to guide the VLA. Across three simulated bimanual tasks and a real-world box pickup task, our method outperforms a VLA fine-tuned only on nominal demonstrations and a VLM-prompting baseline that can remove topology-relevant information from observations. On hardware, it exceeds the strongest baseline by 40% in task success. Project website: https://topology-vla.github.io.</p></details> |  |
| **[AntiGrounding: Executable Robot Trajectories as Visual Prompts for VLM-Guided Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.12374v4)** | 2026-09-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Natural-language instructions specify manipulation goals but leave the robot's motion underdetermined. We present AntiGrounding, a visual action-selection framework built around a dual geometric--visual trajectory interface. Each short trajectory retained after feasibility filtering remains an explicit motion plan and serves as a visual prompt for instruction-conditioned vision--language model (VLM) assessment. Structured multi-view visual question answering (VQA) scores safety, task alignment, efficiency, and physical plausibility. Weighted view fusion aggregates these scores for trajectory selection, while the scores also guide subsequent translational proposals. Separate orientation and gripper controls coordinate physical interaction. Planning proceeds in an initialized digital twin, which validates selected segments before the real robot executes the same waypoint sequences. Across eight real-world manipulation tasks, AntiGrounding with a single GPT-6 Astra evaluator achieves \AstraOverall\% overall success. Under the reported deployment protocol, $π_{0.5}$ achieves \PiOverall\%, and a PIVOT-style visual proposal-selection baseline with the same evaluator achieves \PivotOverall\%. Component ablations and evaluator sensitivity characterize trajectory assessment, proposal search, orientation control, and evaluator choice. Performance depends on digital-twin fidelity and physical interaction.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 7 figures, 3 tables. Submitted to ICRA 2027</p></details> |
| **[InsightChain: Optimized Chain-of-Insight Analytics for LLM-driven Data Visualization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06438v1)** | 2026-09-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for automated data visualization, yet existing approaches often frame visualization generation as a single-step mapping from user query to figure or code, overlooking the iterative analytical reasoning process of expert analysts. To address the evaluation gap for complex data visualization, we introduce the Insight Progression Metric (IPM), a rubric combining four text-based dimensions with a vision-based dimension. We assess IPM through a 100-chain human pilot and an expanded 300-chain agent-based evaluation spanning all ten domains. Experiments on public datasets show that InsightChain consistently outperforms competing prompting baselines. Existing APO methods fail to yield consistent gains on this multi-stage task, whereas VG-COPRO improves performance in both in-domain and cross-domain settings.</p></details> | EMNLP 2026 Findings |
| **[MM-BrowseComp: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Multimodal Browsing Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13186v2)** | 2026-08-31 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents with advanced reasoning and tool-use capabilities have demonstrated impressive performance in web browsing for deep search. However, existing benchmarks such as BrowseComp primarily focus on textual content, overlooking the prevalence of multimodal content. To bridge this gap, we introduce MM-BrowseComp, a novel benchmark comprising 400 challenging, hand-crafted questions designed to evaluate multimodal retrieval and reasoning capabilities. Unlike prior work, MM-BrowseComp incorporates visual prompts and necessitates the extraction of key evidence from web images and videos to complete questions, rendering text-only approaches insufficient. Additionally, we provide a verified checklist for each question, enabling fine-grained analysis of multimodal dependencies and reasoning paths. Our comprehensive evaluation of 27 state-of-the-art models reveals that even leading models like GPT-5-High with tools achieve only 24.25\% accuracy, highlighting the suboptimal multimodal browsing capabilities, establishing MM-BrowseComp as a rigorous new standard for the field.</p></details> | <details><summary>EMNLP...</summary><p>EMNLP 2026. The first two authors contribute equally, 20 pages, repo at https://github.com/MMBrowseComp/MM-BrowseComp</p></details> |
| **[OPUS: A Simple yet Effective Unified Framework for Open-Vocabulary Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30247v1)** | 2026-08-31 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent unified open-vocabulary detection (OVD) supports heterogeneous prompts, including text queries, visual exemplars, and their combinations, but often rely on increasingly complex designs such as heavy cross-modal fusion, staged training, and iterative annotation pipelines. We revisit whether such complexity is necessary in the era of stronger foundation models. Our finding is that unified OVD can be made substantially simpler with semantic-rich visual representations and scalable grounding supervision. We present OPUS (\textbf{O}pen-vocabulary, \textbf{P}rompt-\textbf{U}nified, \textbf{S}imple), a unified detector supporting text, interactive visual, generic visual, and mixed prompting within one framework. OPUS adopts a simple three-part design. Its model architecture combines a semantic-rich visual encoder, built on a DINOv3-ConvNeXt-B backbone with efficient hybrid encoding, with a prompt-aware decoder that avoids prompt-specific branches for unified prompt reasoning. ### World Models for Autonomous Driving
> World models, future prediction, planning priors, and trajectory reasoning for driving and navigation.

| **[Not all world models can doubt: how training objective and architecture shape epistemic uncertainty in JEPA and RSSM world models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34830v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models predict how an environment responds to actions, so that candidate actions can be evaluated before execution. Executed actions, or decisions, taken in this manner are only as reliable as the model's predictive capabilities, and predictions in turn are only reliable within the model's experience. Epistemic uncertainty (EU) is the signal that marks where that experience ends. In this work we offer an extensive empirical study on how training objectives and architectures shape epistemic uncertainty of world models. More precisely, we present a controlled comparison of post-hoc EU (last-layer Laplace and ensembles) between two dominant models of different paradigms: 1) LeWorldModel (LeWM): A joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA), which predicts the next embedding without a decoder, and 2) DreamerV3: A recurrent state-space model (RSSM), which reconstructs the observation from a recurrent latent state. In ten simulated visual-control environments, we follow a controlled state perturbation of the environment state through every stage of the models up to the final EU estimate. We find that the RSSM attenuates the state perturbation through two mechanisms before it reaches an estimator on the predictor feature. Its recurrent update averages over context, so a change in a single frame reaches the state at a fraction of its size and recovers only as the change persists. Its reconstruction objective weights an entity by the pixels it covers, so a small object can be lost due to the training objective. The JEPA has neither mechanism and passes the change through all stages to the uncertainty. We further show that this translates to gaps in five uncertainty related downstream tasks, from unfamiliar-state detection to uncertainty-penalised planning: the RSSM uncertainty misses deviations that its own posterior registers, whereas the JEPA uncertainty reports what its model sees.</p></details> |  |
| **[Was the Model Given the Right Action? These evaluations assume that actions are prepared the same way as during training, but an unchecked mismatch can make a model appear less accurate than it is. Correcting the inputs, without retraining, reduces mean visual prediction error by 42%, measured with DreamSim on the RECON navigation dataset. The correction also changes the ranking of NWM checkpoints in an exploratory analysis and removes or reduces two alternative models' measured advantages. Correcting only the normalization offset instead increases error, showing why interacting mismatches must be checked together. To test whether sensitivity to input preparation extends beyond NWM, we deliberately introduce encoding errors into two alternative models; both become less accurate. We introduce ActRosetta, a tool that checks whether action encoding matches training, identifies supported causes of disagreement, and repairs inputs when sufficient information is available. Repair helps little where predictions barely respond to actions, the tool's diagnosis has limited coverage, and improved control remains unproven. For any action-conditioned world model, the lesson is the same: before reporting rollout error, verify that evaluation encodes actions exactly as training did.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 8 figures, 10 tables</p></details> |
| **[Do We Still Need Reinforcement Learning When We Can Read the World Model?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34656v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning has achieved remarkable success in sequential decision-making, but learning effective policies often requires substantial interaction experience. Further analyses reveal interpretable decision rules and how they evolve through feedback-driven refinement. These findings suggest that direct policy synthesis can reduce reliance on trial and error, challenging the necessity of task-specific RL training.</p></details> | <details><summary>44 pa...</summary><p>44 pages, 10 figures, 24 tables</p></details> |
| **[$μ$MPC: Microsecond-Scale Nonlinear and World Model Control from Edge to GPU via Koopman-Linearized Precompilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34597v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present $μ$MPC, a fast model predictive control (MPC) framework that reduces online computation using learned Koopman-linearized dynamics. | **[Escaping Latent Traps by Remembering: Training-Free Episodic Kernel Repulsion for World-Model Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22470v2)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Latent world models plan towards a goal image by minimising the distance between predicted and goal latents. | **[WorldUAV: Scaling UAV Data Synthesis via World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33682v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>UAV detection and tracking remain constrained by the scarcity of high-quality training data. Therefore, we introduce WorldUAV, a high-quality UAV dataset constructed through an efficient framework powered by world models. Specifically, WorldUAV directly integrates UAVs into readily available real-world videos, naturally preserving realistic backgrounds, illumination, and scene dynamics. Leveraging the spatiotemporal priors of world models, it generates UAV videos with plausible motion, pose variations, and cross-frame consistency, effectively narrowing the gap between synthetic and real data. We further develop an agentic pipeline orchestrated by a multimodal large language model that acquires sources, plans UAV trajectories, invokes synthesis, and verifies generated videos, enabling fully automated dataset construction. WorldUAV comprises 6,878 UAV videos and 205,778 UAV images. Experiments show that WorldUAV significantly improves the performance of UAV detectors. Moreover, it substantially reduces dataset construction costs, requiring only 15 seconds and USD 0.0005 API cost per high-quality UAV image. | **[Next-Latent Prediction Transformers Learn Compact World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.05963v7)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Transformers replace recurrence with a memory that grows with sequence length and self-attention that enables ad-hoc lookups over past tokens. Specifically, NextLat trains a transformer to learn latent representations that are predictive of its next latent state given the next token. Theoretically, we show that these latents converge towards belief states, compressed information about the history necessary to predict the future. This simple auxiliary objective injects a recurrent inductive bias into transformers while leaving their architecture, parallel training efficiency, and inference unchanged. NextLat effectively encourages transformers to form compact internal world models with coherent belief states and transition dynamics -- crucial properties not guaranteed by standard next-token prediction alone. Empirically, across benchmarks in world modeling, reasoning, planning, and language modeling, NextLat demonstrates significant gains over standard next-token prediction and other baselines in downstream accuracy, representation compression, and lookahead planning. Furthermore, NextLat enables variable-length self-speculative decoding, accelerating inference by up to 3.3x in language modeling. NextLat offers a simple yet effective paradigm for learning compact, predictive representations in transformers that generalize better. Our code is available at https://github.com/JaydenTeoh/NextLat.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[RAN-JEPA: An Action-Conditioned World Model for Energy-efficient Mobile Network Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17584v2)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Operational mobile networks are rich in observations but poor in interventions: they continuously generate large volumes of passive telemetry, while controlled action--response trajectories remain comparatively scarce and costly to collect. | **[Do Latent World Models Really Select the Right Actions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05461v2)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper shows that the planning success of a latent world model does not reveal how well its score selects actions. We make two observations. First, the score is informative but unreliable: it picks better actions than chance, yet it often passes over a successful sequence that the search has already found. | **[Economic World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32663v1)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can artificial intelligence reproduce the economic dynamics that emerge as households, firms and governments interact and adapt through markets and institutions? As an initial implementation, we learn household policies from microeconomic data and calibrate a structural economic transition model using macroeconomic data, linking individual decisions to aggregate dynamics. Our micro-to-macro evaluation framework compares simulated individual actions and group distributions with observed microdata and assesses macroeconomic dynamics through forecasting and counterfactual experiments. Results show that simulated and observed household consumption distributions overlap by 94.9%, while EWM forecasts closely track real GDP and several other aggregate levels. In government-borrowing counterfactuals, the EWM reproduces the benchmark life-cycle model's short-run transmission from borrowing to consumption. For ex ante planning, the EWM enables an LLM agent to achieve 10.29% higher simulated GDP under the epidemic shock than direct LLM decisions. This initial EWM is only a starting point. We invite economists and AI researchers to build a stronger EWM together. Economists can contribute richer economic models and knowledge, while AI researchers can develop stronger data-driven methods for simulating agent behaviour and learning economic transitions. | **[CodeWAM: A Code World Action Model for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32483v1)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Driving world models anticipate action consequences through representations of how the world evolves. | **[Does Latent Planning Survive Point Clouds? ### Embodied AI for Navigation
> Embodied agents, embodied intelligence, and robot navigation methods relevant to autonomous systems.

By day a 27B agent works in a household environment. At night it reads its record, finds many of its steps wasted on replies that reach the token limit before any action, traces the habit to one direction in its hidden states, and rewrites one tensor of its weights. An agent that rewrites itself must first never break itself, so it is its own examiner: it tests every change against its unchanged self on practice tasks and keeps only what holds. When an edit holds, the next day it is a better agent, at the same cost per step: over thousands of held-out episodes, 48 fresh seeds a task, its one kept edit makes it complete significantly more tasks, in fewer steps, with fewer wasted and fewer tokens. This self-improvement is multi-round, stable and continual: through the nights that follow, the upgrade holds, without collapse. Of four sizes of its family, only the 27B agent does this. From here on, an embodied agent need not keep training and inference apart: as it works, it gathers its own experience and upgrades itself, with no separate pre-training or post-training and no human intervention. We believe this opens lifelong continual learning for robots: stable, continual, recursive self-improvement.</p></details> |  |
| **[RUIBench: What Makes a Good Robot-Use Interface?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31115v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Foundation models can reason about complex physical tasks, yet translating this intelligence into reliable robot execution remains challenging. We further develop RUIKit, a unified framework that crosses six action abstractions with two interaction forms, yielding 13 interfaces under shared perception and control. In simulation, changing only the RUI shifts a frontier agent's success rate from 45% to 81%, and that of a fine-tuned 4B model from 13% to 47%. Our analysis reveals that effective interfaces reduce unnecessary decisions while preserving essential feedback, with their benefits shaped by agent behavior and physical uncertainty. We further uncover a verification gap, where agents frequently declare success despite observable failures, revealing the need for task-level verification beyond individual actions. Together, these findings establish RUI design as a critical factor in embodied agent reliability.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project website: https://showlab.github.io/RUIBench</p></details> |
| **[RoboUniverse-10K: Toward the Scaling Era of Agentic Robotics via Real-to-Sim Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31002v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic Robotics, in which foundation models act as coding agents to control robots through executable programs, has emerged as a compelling paradigm for embodied intelligence. | **[WorldLeague: Benchmarking Physical Agency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30989v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI systems can now operate Blender, follow instructions in complex video games, and manipulate objects with robot arms. We introduce \textbf{WorldLeague}, a benchmark that compares AI systems on a common set of \textbf{60 simulated robot tasks} by measuring progress toward each task's physical goal from the robot's trajectory. Robots explore scenes to gather evidence in \textbf{WorldSeek}, rearrange objects in \textbf{WorldShape}, and adapt to changes in \textbf{WorldShift}. Tying these goals to a single control interface limits which AI systems can be compared. In WorldLeague, vision-language models (VLMs) choose targets and motions, while vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs) use their native controls. For each of six capability dimensions, we measure how much progress a system makes toward its goals (Interactive Score) and how often it achieves them (physical success rate). Among 13 representative AI systems, Claude Fable 5.2 achieves 13 of the 60 goals and earns the highest mean Interactive Score (28.58/100). Even when VLMs answer every final question correctly, they receive an Interactive Score of zero in 71.8\% of those episodes. WorldLeague gives Embodied AI, Physical AI, and Agentic AI a shared basis for comparing what AI systems achieve autonomously, from partial progress toward a physical goal to full goal completion. Project Webpage: https://xyangzhou.github.io/WorldLeague</p></details> |  |

| **[Audio-Visual Rearrangement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30171v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce Audio-Visual Rearrangement (AVR), a novel task in which an agent hears a spoken request, retrieves a suitable object, and delivers it to the requesting user or an instruction-specified destination. | **[Spiking World-Action Models for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30041v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World action models (WAMs) predict how the world responds to actions and offer a promising path toward general embodied intelligence, but they are large and run the network many times per plan, so they are hard to deploy on robots. | **[Visual Foresight for Agentic Reasoning Before Action in Urban Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04916v2)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Urban navigation requires embodied agents to pursue long-horizon goals through local decisions based on egocentric observations. | **[JARVIS: An Agent That Designs, Builds, and Tests Circuit Boards on Its Own](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26503v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Power electronics is central to the energy transition, but developing new hardware still relies on human-led design, fabrication, testing, and iteration. | **[Mind2Act World: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Embodied Mind, Physical Action, and Mind-Action Composition in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26444v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied intelligence aims to let robots perform complex physical tasks with human-like adaptability and reliability, which requires three complementary capabilities: history-based cognitive reasoning, precise and timely physical execution, and the composition of reasoning and execution through environmental feedback. | **[EmbodiedLab: Beyond Manipulation, Benchmarking Embodied Agents That Act to Know](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35385v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Physical action not only changes the state of an environment but is also the way to know its hidden properties: what cannot be seen must be inferred from the feedback to the change. | **[Nav4Manip: From Navigation to Dexterous Mobile Manipulation through Co-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34655v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dexterous mobile manipulation is essential for robots to perform useful tasks in complex, unstructured environments. However, existing systems face two challenges. First, navigation remains too coarse for downstream manipulation, which often requires a precise base position and approach orientation rather than merely reaching a meter-scale goal region. Second, navigation and manipulation are commonly trained in isolation, lacking a feedback loop that enables the two capabilities to co-evolve. These limitations prevent current systems from reliably executing long-horizon dexterous mobile-manipulation tasks in real-world environments. To address them, we introduce Nav4Manip, a bidirectionally coupled co-evolution system for long-horizon dexterous mobile manipulation. It comprises two complementary architectures: Nav4Manip-N learns long-horizon navigation and refines its policy through manipulation feedback, while Nav4Manip-M trains a world-action model (WAM) for downstream dexterous manipulation. We train Nav4Manip-N in three stages: multi-task co-tuning learns navigation behavior and manipulation-aware affordance prediction; rejection sampling fine-tuning (RFT) rejects low-quality policy-generated paths and fine-tunes the model on retained trajectories; and reinforcement learning from AI feedback (RLAIF) uses counterfactual preferences to further improve navigation decisions. To support this framework, we establish the Nav-N-Manip benchmark and construct Nav4Manip-Pairs from benchmark rollouts, providing large-scale paired navigation--manipulation trajectories and affordance supervision. Extensive experiments on public vision-language navigation (VLN) benchmarks and Nav-N-Manip demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, while real-world evaluations show that Nav4Manip completes diverse dexterous mobile-manipulation tasks. Project website: https://nav4manip.github.io/.</p></details> |  |
| **[GOOSELoc: A Multimodal Benchmark for Ground-to-Aerial Localization in Unstructured Off-Road Terrain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16162v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cross-view pose estimation estimates a ground camera's geographic pose by matching onboard sensor data with geo-referenced overhead imagery. This is particularly relevant when GNSS is unreliable, for example under forest canopy or in urban canyons. Existing metric cross-view benchmarks, such as VIGOR and cross-view KITTI, are dominated by urban and road scenes with strong human-made structure without off-road terrain. We introduce GOOSELoc, a multimodal benchmark for ground-to-aerial localization in unstructured environments, including forests, fields, and rural tracks. GOOSELoc comprises 9,356 scenes recorded across all four seasons around Munich, Germany, pairing geo-referenced aerial orthophotos with surround-view RGB imagery and 3D LiDAR point clouds. Paved/unpaved labels enable analyzing the impact of terrain structure, while the seasonal recordings allow studying the appearance gap to mostly summer-captured orthophotos. To assess the transferability of existing methods, we adapt and fine-tune four state-of-the-art cross-view localization baselines on GOOSELoc. Despite task-specific fine-tuning, the performance degrades substantially in off-road environments. We therefore propose a multimodal baseline combining surround-view imagery and LiDAR geometry, improving localization performance and providing a stronger reference for future work. Data and baselines are released at https://gooseloc.github.io.</p></details> |  |
| **[CRAFT-E: An Interpretable and Editable Neuro-Symbolic Framework for Affordance-Grounded Active Object Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04231v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Assistive robots operating in unstructured environments must reason not only about what objects are, but how they can be used, including non-canonical and improvised uses. We introduce CRAFT-E, a modular neuro-symbolic framework that composes an LLM-derived verb-property-object knowledge graph with CLIP-based visual alignment and energy-based grasp reasoning. The system exposes interpretable grounding paths, incorporates grasp feasibility into affordance inference, and supports targeted graph edits without retraining. We also construct a benchmark dataset with unified annotations for verb-object compatibility, segmentation, and grasp candidates, and deploy the full pipeline on a physical robot. Across static scenes, real-world trials involving 20 verbs and 39 objects, ImageNet-based functional retrieval, and COCO-Tasks transfer, CRAFT-E provides a competitive training-free prior for affordance-grounded retrieval while exposing component-level diagnostics and graph-level interventions. Under staged and full-pipeline perception, CRAFT-E matches strong flat LLM priors in several settings while adding failure attribution and editable reasoning paths. By coupling symbolic reasoning with embodied perception, CRAFT-E offers an inspectable and customizable framework for affordance-grounded object retrieval.</p></details> | <details><summary>WACV ...</summary><p>WACV 2027. Project page: https://zzc0053.github.io/CRAFT-E-page/</p></details> |
| **[RAGNAROK: Radar-Aided Gravity-Normalized Alignment for Robust Open Keyframe-based Radar-Visual-Kinematic-Inertial SLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11531v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Legged robots offer superior mobility in unstructured environments, but reliable operation in such conditions requires robust state estimation. To address the vulnerability of proprioceptive estimators in rough terrain, recent methods have incorporated radar to provide velocity measurements. In this letter, we present RAGNAROK, the first radar-visual-kinematic-inertial SLAM designed for robust operation in challenging environments. It integrates slip- and rolling-contact-aware leg velocity estimation, a kinematics-aware radar factor, and degradation-aware image enhancement. We further incorporate a B-spline-based radar-aided proprioceptive backbone, adaptive weighting, and online extrinsic calibration. Extensive experiments on public and self-collected datasets demonstrate that RAGNAROK achieves robust performance under challenging conditions and outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. The source code and dataset are available at https://github.com/hanjun815/RAGNAROK.</p></details> |  |
| **[GenZ-LIO: Generalizable LiDAR-Inertial Odometry Beyond Confined--Open Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16273v3)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For field robotic missions such as inspection, search-and-rescue, and exploration, light detection and ranging (LiDAR)-inertial odometry (LIO) can serve as a core component of autonomy by providing localization and mapping in GNSS-denied or unstructured environments. However, transitions between confined and open spaces, which are commonly encountered in field deployments, can induce substantial changes in scan density and local geometric structure, thereby reducing the robustness and computational efficiency of LIO. To address these issues, we present GenZ-LIO, a generalizable LIO framework designed to adapt to variations in spatial scale across confined and open environments. GenZ-LIO comprises three components: (i) scale-aware adaptive voxelization for regulating scan downsampling across spatial-scale changes, (ii) hybrid-metric state update for combining point-to-plane and point-to-point residuals under varying geometric structure, and (iii) voxel-pruned correspondence search for efficient point-to-point matching. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation using 42 sequences from nine public datasets and our newly collected NarrowWide dataset to analyze LIO performance under spatial-scale variations across diverse field scenarios. Across the evaluated sequences, GenZ-LIO maintains stable odometry estimation without divergence, indicating practical robustness under the tested field conditions. Our code is available at https://github.com/cocel-postech/genz-lio .</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages, 19 figures, Accepted to IEEE Transactions on Field Robotics (T-FR)</p></details> |
| **[AGT-CV: An Aerial-Ground Team Cross-View Dataset for Heterogeneous Robot Teams in Unstructured Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06478v2)** | 2026-10-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Heterogeneous air-ground robot teams combine complementary sensing modalities, mobility characteristics, and spatial viewpoints that can significantly enhance perception in complex outdoor environments. However, progress in multi-robot collaborative perception has been constrained by the lack of real-world datasets featuring overlapping multi-modal observations from platforms operating in unstructured terrain. We present the \textbf{AGT-CV} (\textbf{A}erial-\textbf{G}round \textbf{T}eam \textbf{C}ross-\textbf{V}iew) dataset, a real-world multi-robot collaborative perception dataset collected using a Clearpath Husky UGV and an Autel EVO~II UAV across diverse unstructured environments, including forest trails, rocky paths, muddy terrain, snow piles, and grass-covered fields. The ground platform provides 3D LiDAR, stereo camera, IMU, and GPS data, while the aerial platform contributes RGB imagery, thermal/infrared observations, and GPS from a complementary overhead viewpoint, allowing for rich cross-modal and cross-view perception. The dataset is collected in 4 unique environments, with over 13,000 synchronized frames across approximately 29 minutes of operation, and includes both SAM~3-based zero-shot segmentation and almost 8,000 manually labeled images. A unique aspect of the dataset is its early-spring collection period, during which sparse tree canopies allow the aerial robot to partially observe the ground robot and terrain through the trees, allowing for occlusion-aware collaborative perception. Unlike prior multi-robot datasets that primarily focus on SLAM or simulated cooperative driving, AGT-CV is specifically designed to support research on cross-view perception, air-ground viewpoint fusion, terrain-aware perception, and collaborative scene understanding in real off-road environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>CDEL ...</summary><p>CDEL -- ECCV 2026 Workshops</p></details> |
| **[Continual Field-Adaptive Models (CFAMs) for Post-Deployment Physical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04552v2)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Unattended interactive autonomy - machines that step into danger in place of humans and complete tasks with human tools - remains a missing capability in mission-critical operations. These domains offer scarce training data and only onboard compute, yet deployed systems must face novelty without erasing prior competence. We introduce Continual Field-Adaptive Models (CFAMs), which learn efficiently in the lab and continue learning after deployment through autonomous, gradient-free, on-device updates. CFAM uses a complementary learning architecture with a frozen slow-learning component and a fast-learning Capsule Field. The slow component contains three cortices: Sensor, which maps multimodal input into 3D-grounded geometry; Reasoning, which decomposes tasks into skills and evaluates outcomes; and Action, which executes geometric skills. The Capsule Field stores field learning one-shot and gradient-free as Competence Capsules. Skill installation is few-shot in the lab and continual in the field; open-world novelty is outside scope. We evaluate CFAM across five embodiments: manipulator, quadruped, humanoid, quadrotor, and off-road vehicle. Baselines (pi0, CogACT, SpatialVLA) use the same in-house multi-embodiment dataset for physical-platform comparisons. CFAM reaches the operating point of a standard policy trained on the full prior-training dataset using 40% of the data, or 2.5x fewer trajectories. At test time, autonomous capture of verified near-OOD cases improves action success by 13.9 percentage points. In sequential simulation, backward transfer is -0.5 percentage points versus -11.4 for LoRA. CFAM therefore provides a bounded form of post-deployment physical intelligence: few-shot skill learning, autonomous field growth from verified near-OOD experience, and retention of prior competence.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rectify, Don't Regret: On-Policy Closed-Loop Training for Multimodal Trajectory Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23393v2)** | 2026-09-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current trajectory prediction models are primarily trained in an open-loop manner, which often leads to covariate shift and compounding errors when deployed in real-world, closed-loop settings. In this work, we propose a closed-loop training paradigm for receding-horizon ego prediction and identify two design choices that are critical for it to succeed. First, closed-loop supervision must be on-policy: each predicted mode is rolled out and supervised only from the states its own execution produced, preserving the prediction accuracy that cross-mode supervision would otherwise degrade. Second, gradients must not flow across the executed state transition, as backpropagating through these transitions allows the model to retroactively adjust an earlier prediction so that the resulting state is easier to predict from, mathematically regretting a mistake rather than learning to recover from it. We show empirically that this shortcut couples the learned policy to the specific replanning rate used during training and degrades at other rates, whereas detaching the gradient forces the model to rectify drift instead, allowing it to generalize across replanning rates unseen during training. Evaluated across three benchmarks (nuScenes, DeepScenario, and Argoverse 2) and two architectures, a decoder-only adaptation of LMFormer and an unmodified implementation of QCNet, our approach matches open-loop baselines under single-shot evaluation and improves with higher replanning rates, reducing collisions by up to 35% and off-road rates by up to 67%.</p></details> |  |
| **[RoSe-SLAM: Robust Semantic-Aware Gaussian Splatting SLAM from Dynamic Monocular Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29003v1)** | 2026-08-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In dynamic and unstructured environments, conventional SLAM systems generally suffer from significant accuracy degeneration due to their static assumptions. In this work, we propose Robust Semantic-aware Gaussian Splatting SLAM (RoSe-SLAM), to address the dynamic challenge by a holistic semantic scene understanding from uncalibrated monocular inputs, achieving accurate camera tracking and high-quality geometry reconstruction. Unlike conventional semantic SLAM using handcrafted semantic labels, our RoSe-SLAM exploits the semantic feature from 2D foundation model to enhance the dynamic tracking and mapping performance. By distilling the rich semantic features to our Gaussian fields, our method effectively identifies dynamic distractors and achieves semantic-aware multi-view consistency, significantly enhancing the geometric reconstruction and scene inpainting. Specifically, we propose a spatial-temporal motion mask generation module, enabling both long-term motion monitoring and short-term transient dynamics capturing, achieving robust and effective disentanglement of dynamic objects and static backgrounds. During global bundle adjustment, we propose an occlusion-aware keyframe selection mechanism to prioritize the occlusion as metric to pick the keyframes, and a multi-view semantic consistency module to improve the mapping quality in dynamic environments. By combining geometric motion cues with semantic priors, our system dynamically filters unreliable observations and reconstructs accurate static scene geometry. Extensive experiments conducted on benchmark datasets including dynamic TUM, Bonn and Wild-Mocap datasets, demonstrate that our method achieves superior performance in both trajectory estimation and static scene mapping, outperforming existing dynamic RGB SLAM baselines in long-term dynamic indoor environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by IEEE/RSJ INTERNATIONAL CONFERENCE ON INTELLIGENT ROBOTS & SYSTEMS (IROS), 2026</p></details> |
| **[Towards a Unified Understanding of Robot Manipulation: A Comprehensive Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10903v2)** | 2026-08-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied intelligence has witnessed remarkable progress in recent years, driven by advances in computer vision, natural language processing, and the rise of large-scale multimodal models. Among its core challenges, robot manipulation stands out as a fundamental yet intricate problem, requiring the seamless integration of perception, planning, and control to enable interaction within diverse and unstructured environments. This survey presents a comprehensive overview of robotic manipulation, encompassing foundational background, task-organized benchmarks and datasets, and a unified taxonomy of existing methods. We extend the classical division between high-level planning and low-level control by broadening high-level planning to include language, code, motion, affordance, and 3D representations, while introducing a new taxonomy of low-level learning-based control grounded in training paradigms such as input modeling, latent learning, and policy learning. Furthermore, we provide the first dedicated taxonomy of key bottlenecks, focusing on data collection, utilization, and generalization, and conclude with an extensive review of real-world applications. Compared with prior surveys, our work offers both a broader scope and deeper insight, serving as an accessible roadmap for newcomers and a structured reference for experienced researchers. All related resources, including research papers, open-source datasets, and projects, are curated for the community at https://github.com/BaiShuanghao/Awesome-Robotics-Manipulation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Top-down Traffic Scenario Generation via Joint Initial-Goal Diffusion and Trajectory Infilling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11407v1)** | 2026-08-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robust traffic simulators are crucial for developing and testing autonomous vehicles to reduce the costly, labor-intensive real-world data collection process and the need for physical presence on the road. However, existing simulators require agents' initial states to generate trajectories, which limits scalability and diversity due to restrictions on the given initial states. While data-driven agent initialization has been widely studied, the generated initial states are not interpretable in terms of why the agents are initialized at those specific locations. Given known initial states, trajectory generation is also a challenging problem, as the model must learn the variability of the destination and how agents should reach it over time. In this paper, we propose TrafficDiffuser, a top-down traffic scenario generation framework that generates high-level traffic scenarios, defined by initial and goal state pairs, by jointly modeling them. The high-level scenario generation makes initial states better interpretable and reduces trajectory generation into as simple as an infilling problem. We demonstrate how the generated high-level traffic scenarios can be used, including constraining based on different trajectory modes and integrating them with existing trajectory generation models. We conduct extensive experiments on the Argoverse 2 motion prediction dataset to evaluate how well the generated outputs capture real-world distributions. In addition to generating goal states, TrafficDiffuser outperforms the next-best approach for agent initialization, reducing speed distribution distance by 55.3% and the off-road rate by 2.8%.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication at the IEEE International Conference on Intelligent Transportation Systems (ITSC), 2026</p></details> |
| **[UAV-ON: A Benchmark for Open-World Object Goal Navigation with Aerial Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00288v5)** | 2026-07-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aerial navigation is a fundamental yet underexplored capability in embodied intelligence, enabling agents to operate in large-scale, unstructured environments where traditional navigation paradigms fall short. However, most existing research follows the Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) paradigm, which heavily depends on sequential linguistic instructions, limiting its scalability and autonomy. To address this gap, we introduce UAV-ON, a benchmark for large-scale Object Goal Navigation (ObjectNav) by aerial agents in open-world environments, where agents operate based on high-level semantic goals without relying on detailed instructional guidance as in VLN. UAV-ON comprises 14 high-fidelity Unreal Engine environments with diverse semantic regions and complex spatial layouts, covering urban, natural, and mixed-use settings. It defines 1270 annotated target objects, each characterized by an instance-level instruction that encodes category, physical footprint, and visual descriptors, allowing grounded reasoning. These instructions serve as semantic goals, introducing realistic ambiguity and complex reasoning challenges for aerial agents. To evaluate the benchmark, we implement several baseline methods, including Aerial ObjectNav Agent (AOA), a modular policy that integrates instruction semantics with egocentric observations for long-horizon, goal-directed exploration. Empirical results show that all baselines struggle in this setting, highlighting the compounded challenges of aerial navigation and semantic goal grounding. UAV-ON aims to advance research on scalable UAV autonomy driven by semantic goal descriptions in complex real-world environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ACM MM 2025</p></details> |
| **[NEUROSYMLAND: Neuro-Symbolic Landing-Site Assessment for Robust and Edge-Deployable UAV Autonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02277v2)** | 2026-07-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safe landing-site assessment in unstructured environments remains a key challenge for autonomous UAV deployment, as vision-only learning approaches often degrade under terrain variability and provide limited transparency in safety decisions.
